over a wide range of adjustment times.

5
Analysis of the low-temperature Ising STMDP

In this section we provide a more formal analysis of the low-temperature Ising STMDP and prove our main result, i.e., Theorem
3.1
.
Section
5.1
introduces some additional notation and terminology required for the complete analysis of the low-temperature Ising STMDP. In Section
5.2.1
, we formalize the construction of the auxiliary MDP and show that it is indeed an accurate approximation of the original STMDP. Finally, Section
5.2.2
reports an outline of the proof of Theorem
3.1
. Some details of the proofs are deferred to the Supplementary Material.

5.1
Additional notation and terminology

In order to formalize the analysis outlined in Section
3.1
, we introduce some additional notation and terminology.

Given a spin
i\in V
, let
N_{h}(i)\subseteq V
and
N_{v}(i)\subseteq V
denote the sets of its horizontal neighbors and its vertical neighbors. We define the following distance measure
\delta:V\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}
on the lattice:

\delta((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))=\min\{|x^{\prime}-x|,N-|x^{\prime}-x|\}+\min\{|y^{\prime}-y|,N-|y^{\prime}-y|\},

where
(x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in V\times V
, taking into account a torus edge correction.
Furthermore, letting
P(V)
denote the power set of
V
, we define a distance measure
\tilde{\delta}:V\times P(V)\rightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}
as

\tilde{\delta}((x,y),W)=\min_{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in V}\delta((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})),\quad(x,y)\in V,\quad W\subseteq V.

Let
\mu((x,y),W)
denote the set of spins in
W
that are closest to
(x,y)
with respect to this distance measure, i.e.,

\mu((x,y),W)=\argmin_{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in W}\delta((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})),\quad(x,y)\in V,\quad W\subseteq V.

In addition, we define a horizontal distance measure
\delta_{h}:V\times V\rightarrow\mathbb{N}^{\infty}_{0}
as

\delta_{h}((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))=\begin{cases}\min\{|x^{\prime}-x|,N-|x^{\prime}-x|\},&\text{if }y=y,^{\prime}\\
\infty,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Accordingly, let
\tilde{\delta}_{h}:V\times P(V)\rightarrow\mathbb{N}^{\infty}_{0}
be defined as

\tilde{\delta}_{h}((x,y),W)=\min_{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in V}\delta_{h}((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})),\quad(x,y)\in V,\quad W\subseteq V,

and
\mu_{h}((x,y),W)
,
(x,y)\in V
,
W\subseteq V
as

\mu_{h}((x,y),W)=\begin{cases}\emptyset,&\text{if }\delta_{h}((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))=\infty\\
&\text{for all }(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in W,\\
\argmin_{(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in W}\delta_{h}((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})),&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

The vertical counterparts
\delta_{v}
,
\tilde{\delta}_{v}
and
\mu_{v}
are defined in an analogous way.

Given a configuration
\sigma\in S
, let
V_{+}(\sigma)\subseteq V
denote the set of spins in state
+1
in
\sigma
, i.e.,
V_{+}(\sigma)=\{i\in V|\sigma(i)=+1\}
. Furthermore, let
R(\sigma)
denote the spins in the smallest rectangle that circumscribes the set of
+
-spins. We define the set of
corner spins
, the sets of
horizontal and vertical boundary spins
and the set of
interior spins
of
\sigma
as

\displaystyle C(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\left\{i\in V|\sigma(i)=+1,\sum_{j\in N_{h}(i)}\sigma(j)=0\text{ and }\sum_{j\in N_{v}(i)}\sigma(j)=0\right\},

\displaystyle B_{h}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\left\{i\in V|\sigma(i)=+1,\sum_{j\in N_{h}(i)}\sigma(j)=2\text{ and }\sum_{j\in N_{v}(i)}\sigma(j)=0\right\},

\displaystyle B_{v}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\left\{i\in V|\sigma(i)=+1,\sum_{j\in N_{v}(i)}\sigma(j)=2\text{ and }\sum_{j\in N_{h}(j)}\sigma(j)=0\right\},

\displaystyle I(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\left\{i\in V|\sigma(i)=\sigma(j)=+1\text{ for all }j\in N(i)\right\}.

Let
\hat{\sigma}\in S
denote the configuration obtained from
\sigma
by setting all spins in
R(\sigma)
to
+1
, i.e.,

\hat{\sigma}(i)=\begin{cases}+1,&\text{if }i\in R(\sigma),\\
\sigma(i),&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}\quad i\in V.

We proceed to introduce some notation and terminology specific to the low-temperature behaviour of the Ising STMDP.
Let the low-temperature Metropolis dynamics be defined as

\tilde{p}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})=\lim_{\beta\rightarrow\infty}p_{\beta}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}),\quad\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in S.

We call a configuration
\sigma^{\prime}\in S
a
downhill configuration
of a configuration
\sigma\in S
if there exists a sequence
\omega=(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{\ell})
for some
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
that satisfies
\sigma_{0}=\sigma
,
\sigma_{\ell}=\sigma^{\prime}
,
H(\sigma_{t+1})\leq H(\sigma_{t})
and
\sum\limits_{i\in V}|\sigma_{t+1}(i)-\sigma_{t}(i)|\leq 1
for all
t=1,\ldots,\ell-1
. Let the probability of following this sequence under the low-temperature Metropolis dynamics be denoted by
\tilde{p}(\omega)
, i.e.,

\tilde{p}(\omega)=\prod\limits_{t=1}^{\ell-1}\tilde{p}(\sigma_{t},\sigma_{t+1}).

Note that, using this formulation, a configuration
\sigma^{\prime}\in S
is a downhill configuration of
\sigma\in S
if and only if there exists a sequence
\omega=(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{\ell})
for some
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\sigma_{0}=\sigma
,
\sigma_{\ell}=\sigma^{\prime}
and
\tilde{p}(\omega)>0
. Similarly, we define
\sigma^{\prime}\in S
to be an
\ell
-step downhill configuration
of
\sigma\in S
if there exists a sequence
\omega=(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{\ell})
of specified length
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
that satisfies
\sigma_{0}=\sigma
,
\sigma_{\ell}=\sigma^{\prime}
and
H(\sigma_{t+1})\leq H(\sigma_{t})
and
\sum\limits_{i\in V}|\sigma_{t+1}(i)-\sigma_{t}(i)|\leq 1
for all
t=0,\ldots,\ell-1
. Let
\Gamma(\sigma)\subseteq S
denote the set of all downhill configurations of
\sigma\in S
. Similarly, let
\Gamma_{\ell}(\sigma)\subseteq S
denote the set of all
\ell
-step downhill configurations of
\sigma\in S
, where
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.

We call a sequence of configurations
\omega=(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{\ell})
,
\sigma_{0},\ldots,\sigma_{\ell}\in S
, a
downhill path
if
H(\sigma_{t+1})\leq H(\sigma_{t})
and
\sum\limits_{i\in V}|\sigma_{t+1}(i)-\sigma_{t}(i)|=1
for all
t=0,\ldots,\ell-1
. For any
\sigma,\eta\in S
, let
\Omega(\sigma,\eta)
denote the collection of all downhill paths leading from configuration
\sigma
to
\eta
. Furthermore, for any
\sigma\in S
, let
\Omega(\sigma)
denote the set of all downhill paths starting at configuration
\sigma
. Finally, let
\Omega
denote the set of all downhill paths. Hence,
\Omega=\cup_{\sigma\in S}\Omega(\sigma)
and
\Omega(\sigma)=\cup_{\eta\in S}\Omega(\sigma,\eta)
,
\sigma\in S
. For a set
W\subseteq V
, let
W^{*}
denote the set of sequences of any length with elements in
W
. Given a downhill path
\omega=(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{\ell})
, let
\zeta(\omega)=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{\ell})\in V^{*}
denote the corresponding sequence of spins that are flipped along this path, i.e.,
x_{k}
is the spin that was flipped to obtain configuration
\sigma_{k}
from configuration
\sigma_{k-1}
,
k=1,\ldots,\ell
. We now define
\Theta(\sigma,\eta)
,
\Theta(\sigma)
,
\Theta\subseteq V^{*}
,
\sigma,\eta\in S
, as the sets of
downhill sequences
corresponding to the paths in
\Omega(\sigma,\eta)
,
\Omega(\sigma)
and
\Omega
. Note that
\zeta:\Omega\rightarrow\Theta
is a one-to-one mapping from the set of downhill paths to the set of sequences.

We call a spin
i\in V

susceptible
in a configuration
\sigma\in S
if
H(\sigma^{i})\leq H(\sigma)
. Note that a spin
i
is susceptible in
\sigma
if and only if
\tilde{p}(\sigma,\sigma^{i})>0
. Recall that in our setting, we imposed a small external magnetic field
h\in(0,1)
. This implies that a spin in state
+1
is susceptible if and only if it has at least three neighboring spins in state -1. A spin in state -1, on the other hand, is susceptible if and only if it has at least two neighboring spins in state +1. Figure
8
shows an example of a susceptible spin in state
+1
. The set of susceptible spins in a configuration
\sigma\in S
is denoted by
\Delta(\sigma)
.

Figure 8:
Configuration drawn on the dual lattice. Each orange square corresponds to a spin in state
+1
and each white square represents a spin in state
-1
. The dark orange spin, which occupies state
+1
, is susceptible. After all, it has three neighbors in state
-1
, so flipping this spin would lead to a lower energy configuration.

We call a configuration
\sigma\in S

fragile
if at least one spin is susceptible in
\sigma
, that is, if
\Delta(\sigma)\neq\emptyset
. A configuration that is not fragile, we call
robust
. Some examples of fragile and robust configurations are depicted in Figure
9
. Let
U\subseteq S
denote the set of robust configurations. We define
U^{1}
to be the set of robust configurations that are not the all-minus configuration, denoted by
\sigma^{-}
, and in which the spins in state
+1
form a single cluster. Theorem
5.2
characterizes these configurations geometrically as configurations in which the spins in state
+1
form a rectangle.

Figure 9:
Examples of fragile and robust configurations.

Let
U(\sigma)\subseteq U
denote the set of robust configurations that are downhill configurations of
\sigma\in S
, i.e.,
U(\sigma)=U\cap\Gamma(\sigma)
. Accordingly, let
U_{\ell}(\sigma)=U\cap\Gamma_{\ell}(\sigma)
denote the set of robust configurations that are
\ell
-step downhill configurations of
\sigma
for some
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. The sets
U^{1}(\sigma)
and
U^{1}_{\ell}(\sigma)
for
\sigma\in S
and
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
are defined in an analogous way, replacing
U
with
U^{1}
.

We call a downhill path
\omega=(\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{\ell})
,
\sigma_{0},\ldots,\sigma_{\ell}\in S
, a
closed downhill path
if it ends in a robust configuration, i.e., if
\sigma_{\ell}\in U
. We refer to the corresponding sequence of spins
\zeta(\omega)
as a
closed downhill sequence
. Let
\tilde{\Omega}(\sigma,\eta)
,
\tilde{\Omega}(\sigma)
and
\tilde{\Omega}
denote the sets of closed downhill paths, defined analogously to
\Omega(\sigma,\eta)
,
\Omega(\sigma)
and
\Omega
, and let
\tilde{\Theta}(\sigma,\eta)
,
\tilde{\Theta}(\sigma)
and
\tilde{\Theta}
denote the corresponding sets of closed downhill sequences.

Given a sequence
\mathbf{x}\in V^{*}
and a configuration
\sigma\in S
, let
\sigma^{\mathbf{x}}
denote the configuration obtained after consecutively flipping the spins in
\mathbf{x}
from
\sigma
. For a set
W\subseteq V
and a sequence
\mathbf{x}\in V^{*}
, let
\mathbf{x}^{W}
denote the subsequence of
\mathbf{x}
that consists only of those elements of
\mathbf{x}
that are part of
W
. We refer to
\mathbf{x}^{W}
as the
restriction of

\mathbf{x}
to
W
. We now call a set
W\subseteq V

essential
for a configuration
\sigma\in S
if it satisfies the following two requirements:

•

All closed downhill sequences
\mathbf{x}\in\tilde{\Theta}(\sigma)
that have the same restriction to
W
lead to the same robust configuration, i.e., if
\mathbf{x}^{W}=\mathbf{y}^{W}
, for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\tilde{\Theta}(\sigma)
, then
\sigma^{\mathbf{x}}=\sigma^{\mathbf{y}}
.

•

The susceptibility of the spins in
W
only depends on the evolution of the configuration on
W
, that is, for any downhill sequence
\mathbf{x}\in\Theta(\sigma)
, we have
\Delta(\sigma^{\mathbf{x}})\cap W=\Delta(\sigma^{\mathbf{x}^{W}})\cap W
.

The above properties imply that the robust configuration at the end of a closed downhill path is fully determined by the evolution of the spins in the essential set. Furthermore, the dynamics governing the spins in the essential set, starting from the configuration
\sigma
, is also fully specified by the evolution of the spins in the set. As a consequence, when computing transition probabilities to the next robust configuration from a configuration
\sigma
, it suffices to focus on the essential set for
\sigma
, which greatly simplifies computations.

5.2
Analysis of the low-temperature Ising STMDP

Using the concepts defined in Section
5.1
, we now proceed to formalize the analysis of the low-temperature Ising STMDP. First, observe that for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\tilde{P}_{\ell}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,d(\sigma))=0
for all
\sigma^{\prime}\notin\Gamma_{\ell}(\sigma^{d(\sigma)})
. Recalling in addition that the value function is finite, due to the fact that the reward function is bounded, the equations in expression (
15
) reduce to

\tilde{v}^{\pi}_{\lambda,\kappa}(\sigma)=r(\sigma,d(\sigma))+\lambda^{\kappa}\sum\limits_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{\kappa}(\sigma^{d(\sigma)})}\tilde{P}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,d(\sigma))\tilde{v}^{\pi}_{\lambda,\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}),\quad\sigma\in S.

(17)

We show that dropping the contributions of fragile configurations in the summation in expression (
17
) in fact leads to a valid approximation of the low-temperature value function. To this end, we define a transition probability kernel
Q_{\kappa}:S\times A\times S\rightarrow[0,1]
by conditioning on the event that
\sigma_{\kappa}\in U
, given a starting configuration
\sigma\in S
and action
a\in A
, i.e.,

Q_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,a):=\begin{cases}\dfrac{\tilde{P}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,a)}{\sum\limits_{\sigma^{\prime\prime}\in U}\tilde{P}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime\prime}|\sigma,a)},&\text{if }\sigma^{\prime}\in U,\\
0,&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

(18)

for
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in S
,
a\in A
.
In analogy to expression (
16
), we define the operator
\mathcal{G}_{\lambda,\kappa}^{\pi}:\mathbb{R}^{|S|}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{|S|}
as

\mathcal{G}^{\pi}_{\lambda,\kappa}\mathbf{x}:=\mathbf{r}^{\pi}+\lambda^{\kappa}\mathbf{Q}^{\pi}_{\kappa}\mathbf{x},\quad\textbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{|S|}.

(19)

Theorem
5.1
asserts that the fixed point of the operator
\mathcal{G}^{\pi}_{\lambda,\kappa}
can approach the low-temperature value function
\tilde{v}^{\pi}_{\lambda,\kappa}
arbitrarily closely and that the accuracy of this approximation can be controlled by selecting a sufficiently long adjustment time
\kappa
.

Theorem 5.1
.

Consider the operators
\mathcal{F}^{\pi}_{\lambda,\kappa}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\pi}_{\lambda,\kappa}
defined in expressions (
16
) and (
19
) respectively. For all
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a positive integer
K
such that for all
\kappa\geq K
, we have

||\mathcal{F}_{\lambda,\kappa}^{\pi}\mathbf{x}-\mathcal{G}_{\lambda,\kappa}^{\pi}\mathbf{x}||_{\infty}<\varepsilon||\mathbf{x}||_{\infty}\quad\text{for all }\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{|S|}\text{ and }\pi\in\Pi.

Proof.

Let
\pi=d^{\infty}\in\Pi
,
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{|S|}
and
\varepsilon>0
. We start by showing that there exists a positive integer
K
such that for all
\kappa\geq K
, we have

\sum\limits_{\sigma^{\prime}\in U}\tilde{P}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,a)\geq 1-\dfrac{\varepsilon}{2}\quad\text{for all }\sigma\in S,a\in A.

(20)

First, observe that
0<h<1
implies that flipping a susceptible spin strictly decreases the energy of a configuration. Since the energy cannot drop below its global minimum
H_{\text{min}}=-N^{2}(1+h)
, each closed downhill path has finite length. Let
L
denote the maximum length of such a path. Note that the probability of transitioning from a fragile configuration
\sigma\in S
to a different configuration in the low-temperature limit is given by

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma^{\prime}\in S\\
\sigma^{\prime}\neq\sigma\end{subarray}}\tilde{p}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})=\dfrac{|\Delta(\sigma)|}{N^{2}}.

Since each fragile configuration has at least 1 susceptible spin, the probability of taking a step along a downhill path is at least
1/N^{2}
. The minimum number of steps necessary to reach a robust configuration is at most
L
. Hence, we can lower bound the expression
\sum\limits_{\sigma^{\prime}\in U}\tilde{P}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,a)
by the probability of obtaining at least
L
successes in
\kappa
Bernoulli trials with success probability
1/N^{2}
. That is,

\sum\limits_{\sigma^{\prime}\in U}\tilde{P}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,a)\geq\mathbb{P}(X\geq L),\quad\text{for all }\sigma\in S,\quad a\in A,

where
X\sim\text{Bin}\left(\kappa,1/N^{2}\right)
and
\kappa\geq L
. Hence,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\sigma^{\prime}\in U}\tilde{P}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,a)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\mathbb{P}(X<L)=1-\sum\limits_{i=0}^{L-1}\binom{\kappa}{i}\left(\dfrac{1}{N^{2}}\right)^{i}\left(1-\dfrac{1}{N^{2}}\right)^{\kappa-i}.

For a given
i=0,\ldots,L-1
, we have

0\leq\lim_{\kappa\rightarrow\infty}\binom{\kappa}{i}\left(\dfrac{1}{N^{2}}\right)^{i}\left(1-\dfrac{1}{N^{2}}\right)^{\kappa-i}\leq\lim_{\kappa\rightarrow\infty}\dfrac{\kappa^{i}}{i!}\left(\dfrac{1}{N^{2}}\right)^{i}e^{(\kappa-i)\log{(1-1/N^{2})}}=0.

Hence,

\lim_{\kappa\rightarrow\infty}\left[1-\sum\limits_{i=0}^{L-1}\binom{\kappa}{i}\left(\dfrac{1}{N^{2}}\right)^{i}\left(1-\dfrac{1}{N^{2}}\right)^{\kappa-i}\right]=1.

This implies that there exists a positive integer
K\geq L
such that expression (
20
) is satisfied for all
\kappa\geq K
.

We have

||\tilde{\mathbf{P}}^{\pi}_{\kappa}-\mathbf{Q}^{\pi}_{\kappa}||_{\infty}=\max_{\sigma\in S}\sum\limits_{\sigma^{\prime}\in S}\left|\tilde{P}^{\pi}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,d(\sigma))-Q^{\pi}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,d(\sigma))\right|.

Since,
\tilde{P}^{\pi}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,d(\sigma))=Q^{\pi}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,d(\sigma))=0
for all
\sigma\in S
,
\sigma^{\prime}\in S\setminus\Gamma_{\kappa}(\sigma^{d(\sigma)})
and
Q^{\pi}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,d(\sigma))=0
for all
\sigma\in S
and
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{\kappa}(\sigma^{d(\sigma)})\setminus U_{\kappa}(\sigma^{d(\sigma)})
, we obtain

\displaystyle||\tilde{\mathbf{P}}^{\pi}_{\kappa}-\mathbf{Q}^{\pi}_{\kappa}||_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\max_{\sigma\in S}\sum\limits_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{\kappa}(\sigma^{d(\sigma)})}\left|\tilde{P}^{\pi}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,d(\sigma))-Q^{\pi}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,d(\sigma))\right|

(21)

\displaystyle=\max_{\sigma\in S}\Big\{\sum\limits_{\sigma^{\prime}\in U_{\kappa}(\sigma^{d(\sigma)})}\left|\tilde{P}^{\pi}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,d(\sigma))-Q^{\pi}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,d(\sigma))\right|+\sum\limits_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{\kappa}(\sigma^{d(\sigma)})\setminus U_{\kappa}(\sigma^{d(\sigma)})}\tilde{P}^{\pi}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,d(\sigma))\Big\}.

For the first summation, using expression (
18
) yields

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\sigma^{\prime}\in U_{\kappa}(\sigma^{d(\sigma)})}\left|\tilde{P}^{\pi}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,d(\sigma))-Q^{\pi}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,d(\sigma))\right|

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\sigma^{\prime}\in U_{\kappa}(\sigma^{d(\sigma)})}\left|\sum\limits_{\sigma^{\prime\prime}\in U}\tilde{P}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime\prime}|\sigma,d(\sigma))Q^{\pi}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,d(\sigma))-Q^{\pi}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,d(\sigma))\right|

\displaystyle=1-\sum\limits_{\sigma^{\prime}\in U}\tilde{P}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,d(\sigma)).

Choosing
\kappa\geq K
, we now obtain

\sum\limits_{\sigma^{\prime}\in U_{\kappa}(\sigma^{d(\sigma)})}\left|\tilde{P}^{\pi}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,d(\sigma))-Q^{\pi}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,d(\sigma))\right|\leq\dfrac{\varepsilon}{2}.

As for the second summation in expression (
21
), we have, again for
\kappa\geq K
,

\sum\limits_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{\kappa}(\sigma^{d(\sigma)})\setminus U_{\kappa}(\sigma^{d(\sigma)})}\tilde{P}^{\pi}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,d(\sigma))=1-\sum\limits_{\sigma^{\prime}\in U}\tilde{P}_{\kappa}(\sigma^{\prime}|\sigma,d(\sigma))\leq\dfrac{\varepsilon}{2}.

Hence, we obtain

||\tilde{\mathbf{P}}^{\pi}_{\kappa}-\mathbf{Q}^{\pi}_{\kappa}||_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon.

This now implies

||\mathcal{F}^{\pi}_{\lambda,\kappa}\mathbf{x}-\mathcal{G}^{\pi}_{\lambda,\kappa}\mathbf{x}||_{\infty}=||\lambda^{\kappa}(\tilde{\mathbf{P}}^{\pi}_{\kappa}-\mathbf{Q}^{\pi}_{\kappa})\mathbf{x}||_{\infty}\leq\lambda^{\kappa}||\tilde{\mathbf{P}}^{\pi}_{\kappa}-\mathbf{Q}^{\pi}_{\kappa}||_{\infty}\cdot||\mathbf{x}||_{\infty}<\varepsilon||\mathbf{x}||_{\infty},

for all
\kappa\geq K
.
∎

5.2.1
The auxiliary MDP

Theorem
5.1
suggests that the fixed point of the operator
\mathcal{G}^{\pi}_{\lambda,\kappa}
for some
\pi\in\Pi
provides an accurate approximation of the low-temperature value function
\tilde{v}^{\pi}_{\lambda,\kappa}
for a sufficiently long adjustment time
\kappa
. Armed with this result, we construct an auxiliary MDP that serves as a caricature version of the original Ising STMDP and ranges only over the all-minus configuration, denoted by
\sigma^{-}
together with the set
U^{1}
, i.e., the set of robust configurations in which the spins in state
+1
form a single cluster. The following result provides a geometrical characterization of such robust configurations.

Theorem 5.2
.

A configuration
\sigma\in S
belongs to
U^{1}
if and only if
\sigma\neq\sigma^{-}
and the spins in state
+1
form a rectangle of size
i\times j
, for some
i,j\in\{2,3,\ldots,N-3,N-2,N\}
.

Proof.

A configuration
\sigma\neq\sigma^{-}
is robust if and only if each spin in state
-1
has at most one neighbor in state
+1
and each spin in state
+1
has at least two neighbors in state
+1
. A configuration in which the spins in state
+1
form one cluster satisfies these requirements if and only if these spins form a rectangle of size
i\times j
, where
i,j\in\{2,3,\ldots,N-3,N-2,N\}
.
∎

We now formalize the construction of the auxiliary MDP
(\hat{S},\hat{A},\hat{P},\hat{r}
) described in Section
3.1
. Recall that the state space
\hat{S}
consist of 2-dimensional vectors representing the size of a rectangle in the Ising STMDP, i.e.,

\hat{S}=\{(i,j)|i,j=2,3,\ldots,N-3,N-2,N\}\cup\{(0,0)\}.

Here, the vector
(0,0)
corresponds to the all-minus configuration. To formally relate the original low-temperature Ising STMDP to the auxiliary MDP, we define a mapping
\mathcal{I}:U^{1}\cup\{\sigma^{-}\}\rightarrow\hat{S}
as
\mathcal{I}(\sigma)=(i,j)
if
\sigma\in U^{1}
and the spins in state
+1
in
\sigma
form a rectangle of size
i\times j
and
\mathcal{I}(\sigma^{-})=(0,0)
. Given
(i,j)\in\hat{S}
, we now let
\sigma_{(i,j)}\in\mathcal{I}^{-1}((i,j))
, without loss of generality, denote the configuration in
\mathcal{I}^{-1}((i,j))
in which the lower left corner of the rectangle is located at the origin of the lattice.
The action space
\hat{A}_{(i,j)}
corresponding to a state
(i,j)\in\hat{S}
is defined as

\hat{A}(i,j)=\begin{cases}\{0\},&\text{if }i=j=0\text{ or }i=j=N,\\
\{a_{11}^{\prime},a_{12}^{\prime},a_{21}^{\prime},a_{22}^{\prime},a_{0},\tilde{a},0\},&\text{if }i=j=2,\\
\{a_{11}^{\prime},a_{12}^{\prime},a_{21},a_{22},a_{21}^{\prime},a_{22}^{\prime},a_{0},\tilde{a},0\},&\text{if }i=2,\text{ }3\leq j\leq N-3,\\
\{a_{11}^{\prime},a_{21},a_{22},a_{21}^{\prime},a_{22}^{\prime},a_{0},\tilde{a},0\},&\text{if }i=2,\text{ }j=N-2,\\
\{a_{11},a_{12},a_{11}^{\prime},a_{12}^{\prime},a_{21}^{\prime},a_{22}^{\prime},a_{0},\tilde{a},0\},&\text{if }3\leq i\leq N-3,\text{ }j=2,\\
\{a_{11},a_{12},a_{11}^{\prime},a_{12}^{\prime},a_{21}^{\prime},a_{0},\tilde{a},0\},&\text{if }i=N-2,\text{ }j=2,\\
\{a_{11},a_{12},a_{11}^{\prime},a_{12}^{\prime},a_{21},a_{22},a_{21}^{\prime},a_{22}^{\prime},a_{0},\tilde{a},0\},&\text{if }3\leq i,j\leq N-3,\\
\{a_{11},a_{12},a_{11}^{\prime},a_{12}^{\prime},a_{21},a_{21}^{\prime},a_{0},\tilde{a},0\},&\text{if }i=N-2,\text{ }3\leq j\leq N-3,\\
\{a_{11},a_{11}^{\prime},a_{21},a_{22},a_{21}^{\prime},a_{22}^{\prime},a_{0},\tilde{a},0\},&\text{if }3\leq i\leq N-3,\text{ }j=N-2,\\
\{a_{11},a_{11}^{\prime},a_{21},a_{21}^{\prime},a_{0},\tilde{a},0\},&\text{if }i=j=N-2,\\
\{a_{21},0\},&\text{if }i=N-2,\text{ }j=N,\\
\{a_{11},a_{12},0\},&\text{if }i=N,\text{ }2\leq j\leq N-3,\\
\{a_{11},0\},&\text{if }i=N,\text{ }j=N-2,\\
\{a_{21},a_{22},0\},&\text{if }2\leq i\leq N-3,\text{ }j=N.\\
\end{cases}

To clarify the connection to the original STMDP, we define a mapping
\mathcal{J}_{\sigma}:A\rightarrow\hat{A}_{\mathcal{I}(\sigma)}
for each
\sigma\in U^{1}\cup\{\sigma^{-}\}
as

\mathcal{J}_{\sigma}(a)=\begin{cases}a_{1\ell},&\text{if }\tilde{\delta}_{v}(a,V_{+}(\sigma))=\ell\text{ and }\mu_{v}(a,V_{+}(\sigma))\notin C(\sigma),\\
a_{1\ell}^{\prime},&\text{if }\tilde{\delta}_{v}(a,V_{+}(\sigma))=\ell\text{ and }\mu_{v}(a,V_{+}(\sigma))\in C(\sigma),\\
a_{2\ell},&\text{if }\tilde{\delta}_{h}(a,V_{+}(\sigma))=\ell\text{ and }\mu_{h}(a,V_{+}(\sigma))\notin C(\sigma),\\
a_{2\ell}^{\prime},&\text{if }\tilde{\delta}_{h}(a,V_{+}(\sigma))=\ell\text{ and }\mu_{h}(a,V_{+})\in C(\sigma),\\
a_{0},&\text{if }\tilde{\delta}(a,V_{+}(\sigma))=2\text{ and }\tilde{\delta}_{h}(a,V_{+}(\sigma))=\tilde{\delta}_{v}(a,V_{+}(\sigma))=\infty,\\
\tilde{a},&\text{if }a\in C(\sigma),\\
0,&\text{ otherwise,}\end{cases}

for
\ell=1,2
,
a\in A
. Hence,
a_{1\ell}
and
a_{2\ell}
,
\ell=1,2
, correspond to the actions of flipping a spin at distance
\ell
from the horizontal and the vertical side of the rectangle, for which the closest spin belonging to the rectangle is not a corner spin of the rectangle. Actions
a_{1\ell}^{\prime}
,
a_{2\ell}^{\prime}
,
\ell=1,2
, correspond to the actions of flipping a spin at distance
\ell
from the horizontal and the vertical side of the rectangle, where the closest spin belonging to the rectangle is a corner spin. The action
a_{0}
represents the action of flipping a spin that is diagonally adjacent to the rectangle. The action
\tilde{a}
corresponds to the action of flipping a corner spin of the rectangle. The action
0
represents flipping any other spin or doing nothing.
We refer to Figure
1
for a visualization of the action space.

Recall that the reward function of the auxiliary process is given by

\hat{r}(s,a)=\begin{cases}1,&\text{ if }s=(N,N),\\
0,&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

for all
s\in\hat{S}
,
a\in\hat{A}

Finally, we define the transition probability kernel
\hat{P}:\hat{S}\times\hat{A}\times\hat{S}\rightarrow[0,1]
, which is based on the the transition probability kernel
Q_{\kappa}:S\times A\times S\rightarrow[0,1]
given by expression (
18
). To this end, we first provide some preliminary results. The following statement asserts that for any configuration
\sigma\in U^{1}
, action
a\in A
and state
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\hat{S}
, there is at most one robust downhill configuration of
\sigma^{a}
that corresponds to state
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
.

Lemma 5.3
.

For any configuration
\sigma\in U^{1}
, action
a\in A
and state
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\hat{S}
, the set
U^{1}(\sigma^{a})\cap\mathcal{I}^{-1}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))
is either empty or a singleton.

Proof.

Let
\sigma\in U^{1}
and suppose that
\mathcal{I}(\sigma)=(i,j)
. Consider the case that
\mathcal{J}_{\sigma}(a)=0
. This implies that
a\in\{0\}\cup[V_{+}(\sigma)\setminus C(\sigma)]\cup\{i\in V|\tilde{\delta}(a,V_{+}(\sigma))>2\}
. Suppose first that
a=0
. In this case, we have
U^{1}(\sigma^{a})\cap\mathcal{I}^{-1}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))=\{\sigma\}
if
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})=(i,j)
and
U^{1}(\sigma^{a})\cap\mathcal{I}^{-1}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))=\emptyset
otherwise. Suppose now that either
a\in V_{+}(\sigma)\setminus C(\sigma)
or
a
satisfies
\tilde{\delta}(a,V_{+}(\sigma))>2
. In both cases, the only downhill path starting at configuration
\sigma^{a}
is
(\sigma^{a},\sigma)
, so we again obtain
U^{1}(\sigma^{a})\cap\mathcal{I}^{-1}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))=\{\sigma\}
if
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})=(i,j)
and
U^{1}(\sigma^{a})\cap\mathcal{I}^{-1}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))=\emptyset
otherwise.

We proceed to consider the case
\mathcal{J}_{\sigma}(a)=\tilde{a}
. If
i,j\geq 3
, then the only downhill path starting at configuration
\sigma^{a}
is
(\sigma^{a},\sigma)
, which again yields
U^{1}(\sigma^{a})\cap\mathcal{I}^{-1}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))=\{\sigma\}
if
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})=(i,j)
and
U^{1}(\sigma^{a})\cap\mathcal{I}^{-1}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))=\emptyset
otherwise. Suppose now that
a\in C(\sigma)
and either
i=2
,
j>2
or
i>2
,
j=2
. Without loss of generality, we consider the case
i=2
and
j>2
. In this situation,
\sigma^{a}
has two susceptible spins, namely
a
itself and the unique horizontal neighbor
a^{\prime}
of
a
that is in state
+1
. Flipping spin
a
leads back to configuration
\sigma
, whereas flipping spin
a^{\prime}
leads to the robust configuration
\sigma^{\{a,a^{\prime}\}}
, which satisfies
\mathcal{I}(\sigma^{\{a,a^{\prime}\}})=(i,j-1)
. Hence, we obtain

U^{1}(\sigma^{a})\cap\mathcal{I}^{-1}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))=\begin{cases}\{\sigma\},&\text{if }(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})=(i,j),\\
\{\sigma^{\{a,a^{\prime}\}}\},&\text{if }(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})=(i,j-1),\\
\emptyset,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Suppose that
i=j=2
. In this case,
\sigma^{a}
has three susceptible spins, namely
a
, the unique horizontal neighbor
a^{\prime}
of
a
that is in state
+1
and the unique vertical neighbor
a^{\prime\prime}
of
a
that is in state
+1
. If either spin
a^{\prime}
or spin
a^{\prime\prime}
flips first from
\sigma^{a}
, any closed downhill path will end in the all-minus configuration
\sigma^{-}
, which corresponds to state
(0,0)
. On the other hand, flipping spin
a
first leads back to configuration
\sigma
. Thus, we have

U^{1}(\sigma^{a})\cap\mathcal{I}^{-1}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))=\begin{cases}\{\sigma\},&\text{if }(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})=(i,j),\\
\{\sigma^{-}\},&\text{if }(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})=(0,0),\\
\emptyset,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Finally, consider the situation
\mathcal{J}_{\sigma}(a)\neq 0
. In this case, the only spins that can flip on a downhill path starting at configuration
\sigma^{a}
are those in the set
R(\sigma^{a})\setminus V_{+}(\sigma)
, i.e., the spins in the circumscribed rectangle of the set of
+
-spins in
\sigma^{a}
which are not part of the rectangle in configuration
\sigma
. We consider
\mathcal{J}_{\sigma}(a)=a_{12}
. The remaining cases can be dealt with in a similar way. Let
b_{11}(\sigma)
and
b_{12}(\sigma)
denote the horizontal sets of spins lying at distance 1 and 2 from the rectangle in
\sigma
respectively, as depicted in Figure
10
.

Figure 10:
Sets
b_{11}(\sigma)
and
b_{12}(\sigma)
.

We have
R(\sigma^{a})\setminus V_{+}(\sigma)=b_{11}(\sigma)\cup b_{12}(\sigma)
, so the only spins that can flip on a downhill path starting at configuration
\sigma^{a}
are those in the set
b_{11}(\sigma)\cup b_{12}(\sigma)
. This implies that the only robust endpoints of downhill paths starting at
\sigma^{a}
are the configurations
\sigma
itself,
\sigma^{b_{11}(\sigma)}
and
\sigma^{b_{11}(\sigma)\cup b_{12}(\sigma)}
. Thus, we obtain
U^{1}(\sigma^{a})\cap\mathcal{I}^{-1}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))=\{\sigma\}
if
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})=(i,j)
,
U^{1}(\sigma^{a})\cap\mathcal{I}^{-1}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))=\{\sigma^{b_{11}(\sigma)}\}
if
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})=(i,j+1)
,
U^{1}(\sigma^{a})\cap\mathcal{I}^{-1}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))=\{\sigma^{b_{11}(\sigma)\cup b_{12}(\sigma)}\}
if
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})=(i,j+2)
and
U^{1}(\sigma^{a})\cap\mathcal{I}^{-1}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))=\emptyset
otherwise. Analogous results for
\mathcal{J}_{\sigma}(a)\in\{a_{11},a_{21},a_{22},a_{11}^{\prime},a_{12}^{\prime},a_{21}^{\prime},a_{22}^{\prime},a_{0}\}
can be obtained in a similar way. It follows that the set
U^{1}(\sigma^{a})\cap\mathcal{I}^{-1}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))
is either empty or a singleton for any
\sigma\in U^{1}
and
a\in A
.
∎

If the set
U^{1}(\sigma_{(i,j)}^{a})\cap\mathcal{I}^{-1}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))
, for
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\hat{S}
and
a\in A
, is nonempty, let the unique configuration in this set be denoted by
\eta_{\{(i,j),a,(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\}}
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\hat{S}
and
a,a^{\prime}\in A
. If
\mathcal{J}_{\sigma_{(i,j)}}(a)=\mathcal{J}_{\sigma_{(i,j)}}(a^{\prime})
, then

\lim_{\kappa\rightarrow\infty}Q_{\kappa}(\eta_{\{(i,j),a,(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\}}|\sigma_{(i,j)},a)=\lim_{\kappa\rightarrow\infty}Q_{\kappa}(\eta_{\{(i,j),a^{\prime},(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\}}|\sigma_{(i,j)},a^{\prime}).

Proof.

By symmetry, the statement is obvious for

\mathcal{J}_{\sigma_{(i,j)}}(a)=\mathcal{J}_{\sigma_{(i,j)}}(a^{\prime})\in\{a_{11}^{\prime},a_{12}^{\prime},a_{21}^{\prime},a_{22}^{\prime},\tilde{a},a_{0}\}.

We consider
\mathcal{J}_{\sigma_{(i,j)}}(a)=\mathcal{J}_{\sigma_{(i,j)}}(a^{\prime})=a_{12}
. The result for the remaining cases can be obtained in a very similar way. Let the spin
a
be labeled as 1, let its vertical neighbor that is adjacent to the rectangle be labeled as 2 and let the horizontal neighbors of spins 1 and 2 be labeled as 5, 6 and 3, 4 respectively, as illustrated in Figure
11
.

Figure 11:
Post-decision configuration
\sigma_{(i,j)}^{a}
.

Let
b_{11}(\sigma_{(i,j)})
and
b_{12}(\sigma_{(i,j)})
again denote the horizontal sets lying at distance 1 and 2 from the rectangle in
\sigma_{(i,j)}
respectively, as depicted in Figure
10
. Observe first of all that once two adjacent spins in one of these sets reach state
+1
, these spins can no longer flip to
-1
on any downhill path. Furthermore, note that all remaining spins in this set will inevitably occupy state
+1
in any robust configuration at the end of such a downhill path.
The only way to gain two
+
-spins in set
b_{11}(\sigma_{(i,j)})
on a downhill path from configuration
\sigma_{(i,j)}^{a}
is if spin 2 flips first, followed by either spin 3 or spin 4. The only way for set
b_{12}(\sigma_{(i,j)})
to gain two spins on a downhill path from configuration
\sigma_{(i,j)}^{a}
is through flipping either spin 5 or 6, which is only possible if spin 3 or 4 respectively has flipped to
+1
and spin 1 has not flipped back in the mean time. It follows from these arguments that the robust configuration at the end of a closed downhill path is fully determined by the evolution of the spins in the set
W=\{1,2,3,4,5,6\}
. That is, for any two closed downhill sequences
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\tilde{\Theta}(\sigma^{a}_{(i,j)})
that satisfy
\mathbf{x}^{W}=\mathbf{y}^{W}
, we have
(\sigma^{a}_{(i,j)})^{\mathbf{x}}=(\sigma^{a}_{(i,j)})^{\mathbf{y}}
. In addition, the robust configuration we reach after taking action
a
only depends on the rectangle through the spins that are adjacent to spins 2, 3 and 4. Since the configuration of the set of spins labeled 2, 3 and 4 is the same up to translation for each
a\in A
that satisfies
\mathcal{J}_{\sigma_{(i,j)}}(a)=a_{12}
, we can conclude that

\lim_{\kappa\rightarrow\infty}Q_{\kappa}(\eta_{\{(i,j),a,(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\}}|\sigma_{(i,j)},a)=\lim_{\kappa\rightarrow\infty}Q_{\kappa}(\eta_{\{(i,j),a^{\prime},(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\}}|\sigma_{(i,j)},a^{\prime})

if
\mathcal{J}_{\sigma_{(i,j)}}(a)=\mathcal{J}_{\sigma_{(i,j)}}(a^{\prime})=a_{12}
.
∎

We now define the transition probability kernel
\hat{P}:\hat{S}\times\hat{A}\times\hat{S}\rightarrow[0,1]
as

\displaystyle\hat{P}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime})|

\displaystyle(i,j),\hat{a})=\begin{cases}\lim\limits_{\kappa\rightarrow\infty}Q_{\kappa}(\eta_{\{(i,j),a,(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\}}|\sigma_{(i,j)},a),&\text{if }U^{1}(\sigma_{(i,j)}^{a})\cap\mathcal{I}^{-1}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))\neq\emptyset,\\
0,&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

(22)

for
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\hat{S}
,
\hat{a}\in\hat{A}_{(i,j)}
, where
a\in\mathcal{J}^{-1}_{\sigma_{(i,j)}}(\hat{a})
. By Lemma
5.4
, each choice of
a\in\mathcal{J}^{-1}_{\sigma_{(i,j)}}(\hat{a})
yields the same result and therefore expression (
22
) is well-defined.

The following result provides a way to compute the probabilities
\hat{P}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime})|(i,j),\hat{a})
explicitly.

Lemma 5.5
.

Consider
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\hat{S}
and
a\in A
such that
U^{1}(\sigma^{a}_{(i,j)})\cap\mathcal{I}^{-1}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))\neq~\emptyset
. Then,

\displaystyle\lim_{\kappa\rightarrow\infty}Q_{\kappa}(

\displaystyle\eta_{\{(i,j),a,(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\}}|\sigma_{(i,j)},a)=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if }\sigma_{(i,j)}^{a}=\eta_{\{(i,j),a,(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\}},\\
\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega(\sigma_{(i,j)}^{a},\eta_{\{(i,j),a,(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\}})}\prod\limits_{\ell=0}^{|\omega|-1}\dfrac{1}{|\Delta(\omega_{\ell})|},&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(23)

Proof.

Note that a configuration
\sigma\in S
is an absorbing state in the process that evolves according to this dynamics if and only if it is robust. Now, let
\tilde{p}^{\kappa}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})
denote the
\kappa
-step low-temperature Metropolis dynamics and define

\hat{p}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})=\lim_{\kappa\rightarrow\infty}\tilde{p}^{\kappa}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}),\quad\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in S.

It follows that
\hat{p}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})>0
if and only if the configuration
\sigma^{\prime}
is robust. We first show that for any
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in S
that satisfy
\sigma^{\prime}\in U(\sigma)
and
\sigma\neq\sigma^{\prime}
, we have

\hat{p}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})=\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})}\prod\limits_{\ell=0}^{|\omega|-1}\dfrac{1}{|\Delta(\omega_{\ell})|}.

(24)

Recall that
\lim_{\beta\rightarrow\infty}p_{\beta}(\sigma,\sigma^{i})=0
for all
i\notin\Delta(\sigma)
and
\lim_{\beta\rightarrow\infty}p_{\beta}(\sigma,\sigma^{i})=1/N^{2}
for all
i\in~\Delta(\sigma)
. Conditioning on the first time a susceptible spin, if any, is selected by the Metropolis dynamics during the adjustment period yields

\tilde{p}^{\kappa}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})=\sum\limits_{t=1}^{\kappa}\left(1-\dfrac{|\Delta(\sigma)|}{N^{2}}\right)^{t-1}\dfrac{|\Delta(\sigma)|}{N^{2}}\sum\limits_{i\in\Delta(\sigma)}\dfrac{1}{|\Delta(\sigma)|}\tilde{p}^{\kappa-t}(\sigma^{i},\sigma^{\prime}).

Taking the limit
\kappa\rightarrow\infty
, we now obtain

\displaystyle\hat{p}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{t=1}^{\infty}\left(1-\dfrac{|\Delta(\sigma)|}{N^{2}}\right)^{t-1}\dfrac{|\Delta(\sigma)|}{N^{2}}\sum\limits_{i\in\Delta(\sigma)}\dfrac{1}{|\Delta(\sigma)|}\hat{p}(\sigma^{i},\sigma^{\prime})=\sum\limits_{i\in\Delta(\sigma)}\dfrac{1}{|\Delta(\sigma)|)}\hat{p}(\sigma^{i},\sigma^{\prime}).

We show that this implies the validity of expression (
24
) by induction over the length
L(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})
of the longest downhill path that leads from
\sigma
to
\sigma^{\prime}
, i.e.,
L(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})=\max_{\omega\in\Omega(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})}|\omega|-1
. Note that
\Omega(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
and
L(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})\geq 1
, since
\sigma^{\prime}\in U(\sigma)
and
\sigma\neq\sigma^{\prime}
. For
L=1
, we obtain

\hat{p}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})=\sum\limits_{i\in\Delta(\sigma)}\dfrac{1}{|\Delta(\sigma)|}\hat{p}(\sigma^{i},\sigma^{\prime})=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\Delta(\sigma)\\
\sigma^{i}=\sigma^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\dfrac{1}{|\Delta(\sigma)|}=\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})}\prod\limits_{\ell=0}^{|\omega|-1}\dfrac{1}{|\Delta(\omega_{\ell})|}.

Now, assume that expression (
24
) holds if
L\leq n
. For
L=n+1
, we then obtain

\displaystyle\hat{p}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{i\in\Delta(\sigma)}\dfrac{1}{|\Delta(\sigma)|}\hat{p}(\sigma^{i},\sigma^{\prime}).

If
\sigma^{i}=\sigma^{\prime}
, for
i\in\Delta(\sigma)
, then
\hat{p}(\sigma^{i},\sigma^{\prime})=1
. On the other hand, if
\sigma^{i}\neq\sigma^{\prime}
for
i\in\Delta(\sigma)
, then either
\sigma^{\prime}\notin U(\sigma^{i})
or
\sigma^{\prime}\in U(\sigma^{i})
and
L(\sigma^{i},\sigma^{\prime})\leq n
. In the first case, we have
\hat{p}(\sigma^{i},\sigma^{\prime})=0
. In the second case, the induction hypothesis implies that

\hat{p}(\sigma^{i},\sigma^{\prime})=\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega(\sigma^{i},\sigma^{\prime})}\prod\limits_{\ell=0}^{|\omega|-1}\dfrac{1}{|\Delta(\omega_{\ell})|}.

Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\hat{p}(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\Delta(\sigma),\\
\sigma^{i}=\sigma^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\dfrac{1}{|\Delta(\sigma)|}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\Delta(\sigma)\\
\sigma^{i}\neq\sigma^{\prime}\\
\sigma^{\prime}\in U(\sigma^{i})\end{subarray}}\dfrac{1}{|\Delta(\sigma)|}\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega(\sigma^{i},\sigma^{\prime})}\prod\limits_{\ell=0}^{|\omega|-1}\dfrac{1}{|\Delta(\omega_{\ell})|}=\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})}\prod\limits_{\ell=0}^{|\omega|-1}\dfrac{1}{|\Delta(\omega_{\ell})|}.

Now, consider
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\hat{S}
and
a\in A
such that
U^{1}(\sigma^{a}_{(i,j)})\cap\mathcal{I}^{-1}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))\neq\emptyset
.
By definition of the transition probability kernel
Q_{\kappa}
, we have

\lim_{\kappa\rightarrow\infty}Q_{\kappa}(\eta_{\{(i,j),a,(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\}}|\sigma_{(i,j)},a)=\hat{p}(\sigma_{(i,j)}^{a},\eta_{\{(i,j),a,(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\}}).

(25)

First, suppose that
\eta_{\{(i,j),a,(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\}}=\sigma_{(i,j)}^{a}
. Since
\eta_{\{(i,j),a,(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\}}
is a robust configuration, it immediately follows that

\lim_{\kappa\rightarrow\infty}Q_{\kappa}(\eta_{\{(i,j),a,(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\}}|\sigma_{(i,j)},a)=1.

Secondly, suppose that
\eta_{\{(i,j),a,(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\}}\neq\sigma_{(i,j)}^{a}
. It now follows from expression (
24
) that

\lim_{\kappa\rightarrow\infty}Q_{\kappa}(\eta_{\{(i,j),a,(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\}}|\sigma_{(i,j)},a)=\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega(\sigma_{(i,j)}^{a},\eta_{\{(i,j),a,(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\}})}\prod\limits_{\ell=0}^{|\omega|-1}\dfrac{1}{|\Delta(\omega_{\ell})|},

establishing expression (
23
).
∎

Lemma
5.6
now provides the explicit expressions for the transition probabilities

\hat{P}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime})|(i,j),\hat{a})
for each
(i,j),(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\hat{S}
,
\hat{a}\in\hat{A}_{(i,j)}
.

Lemma 5.6
.

The transition probability kernel
\hat{P}:\hat{S}\times\hat{A}\times\hat{S}\rightarrow[0,1]
is given by

\displaystyle\hat{P}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime})|(i,j),a_{11})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1/3,&\text{if }i^{\prime}=i,j^{\prime}=j,\\

0,&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}\quad\text{and }

(49)

\displaystyle\hat{P}((i,j)|(i,j),0)

\displaystyle=1,\quad\text{for all }(i,j)\in\hat{S}.

(50)

Proof.

We give the proof of expression (
28
). Let
(i,j)\in\hat{S}
,
i=3,\ldots,N
,
j=2,\ldots,N-4
and let
a\in\mathcal{J}^{-1}_{\sigma_{(i,j)}}(a_{12})
. Consider again Figure
11
. First, the arguments laid out in the proof of Lemma
5.4
imply that
U^{1}(\sigma^{a}_{(i,j)})\cap\mathcal{I}^{-1}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime}))\neq\emptyset
if and only if
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})=(i,j)
,
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})=(i,j+1)
or
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})=(i,j+2)
. Hence,
\hat{P}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime})|(i,j),a_{12})=0
if
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\notin\{(i,j),(i,j+1),(i,j+2)\}
.

Now, suppose that
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\{(i,j),(i,j+1),(i,j+2)\}
.
The proof of Lemma
5.4
implies that the transition probability
\hat{P}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime})|(i,j),a_{12})
depends only on the evolution of the flipped spin, denoted by
a
, its vertical neighbor that is adjacent to the rectangle, the horizontal neighbors of the latter spin and the horizontal neighbors of
a
itself, labeled 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 and 6 respectively, as illustrated in Figure
11
. Letting
W=\{1,2,3,4,5,6\}
, we showed that for any two closed downhill sequences
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\tilde{\Theta}(\sigma^{a}_{(i,j)})
that satisfy
\mathbf{x}^{W}=\mathbf{y}^{W}
, we have
(\sigma^{a}_{(i,j)})^{\mathbf{x}}=(\sigma^{a}_{(i,j)})^{\mathbf{y}}
. We now proceed to show that the susceptibility of the spins in the set
W=\{1,2,3,4,5,6\}
is not affected by the evolution of the configuration on the remainder of the lattice, i.e., that for any downhill sequence
\mathbf{x}\in\Theta(\sigma)
, we have
\Delta((\sigma^{a}_{(i,j)})^{\mathbf{x}})\cap W=\Delta((\sigma^{a}_{(i,j)})^{\mathbf{x}^{W}})\cap W
. Consider the sequence
\mathbf{y}=(2)
and let
\mathbf{x}\in\Theta(\sigma)
be any sequence that satisfies
\mathbf{x}^{W}=\mathbf{y}
. In configuration
\sigma^{a}_{(i,j)}
, the only susceptible spins are 1 and 2. This implies that the first element of
\mathbf{x}
is spin 2. After flipping spin 2, the set of susceptible spins is
\{1,3,4\}
. Therefore, we have
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{y}=(2)
and thus
\Delta((\sigma^{a}_{(i,j)})^{\mathbf{x}})\cap W=\Delta((\sigma^{a}_{(i,j)})^{\mathbf{x}^{W}})\cap W
. Now consider the sequence
\mathbf{y}=(2,4)
and let
\mathbf{x}\in\Theta(\sigma^{a}_{(i,j)})
again be any sequence that satisfies
\mathbf{x}^{W}=\mathbf{y}
. The same argument as before implies that the first two entries of
\mathbf{x}
are the spins 2 and 4. At this point, the only spins outside of
W
that can flip on any downhill path are the spins in the set
b_{11}(\sigma_{(i,j)})\setminus W
, where
b_{11}(\sigma_{(i,j)})
is the horizontal set lying at distance 1 from the rectangle in
\sigma_{(i,j)}
, as depicted in Figure
10
. Observe, however, that flipping any spins in this set leaves the set of susceptible spins that are part of
W
, namely
\{1,6\}
, unaffected. We thus again obtain
\Delta((\sigma^{a}_{(i,j)})^{\mathbf{x}})\cap W=\Delta((\sigma^{a}_{(i,j)})^{\mathbf{x}^{W}})\cap W
. This argument can easily be extended to the sequence
\mathbf{y}=(2,4,1)
. Since after flipping the spins in this sequence, spins 2 and 4 can no longer flip back on any downhill path and the only spins that will become susceptible on any downhill path are those in the set
b_{11}(\sigma_{(i,j)})
, it follows that any closed downhill sequence
\mathbf{x}\in\tilde{\Theta}(\sigma^{a}_{(i,j)})
that satisfies
\mathbf{x}^{W}=\mathbf{y}
leads to the robust configuration
\sigma_{(i,j)}^{b_{11}(\sigma_{(i,j)})}
.
In Table
28
we listed the restrictions to
W
of all sequences in the set
\tilde{\Theta}(\sigma^{a}_{(i,j)})
. The same argument as above can be applied to any of the sequences in this table. We can thus conclude that for any downhill sequence
\mathbf{x}\in\Theta(\sigma)
, we have
\Delta((\sigma^{a}_{(i,j)})^{\mathbf{x}})\cap W=\Delta((\sigma^{a}_{(i,j)})^{\mathbf{x}^{W}})\cap W
. It follows that this set is essential for the configuration
\sigma_{(i,j)}^{a}
. Thus, to compute
\hat{P}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime})|(i,j),a_{12})
it suffices to focus on the restrictions of the closed downhill sequences to
W
that lead to the robust configuration
\eta_{\{(i,j),a,(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\}}
. Let the set of these sequences be denoted by
\tilde{\Theta}^{W}(\sigma^{a}_{(i,j)},\eta_{\{(i,j),a,(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\}})
. To keep notation simple in the computation that follows, we abbreviate
\sigma^{a}_{(i,j)}
and
\eta_{\{(i,j),a,(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\}}
as
\sigma^{a}
and
\eta
. By the properties of the essential set, it follows that

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\omega\in\tilde{\Omega}(\sigma^{a},\eta)}\prod\limits_{\ell=0}^{|\omega|-1}\dfrac{1}{|\Delta(\omega_{\ell})|}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mathbf{x}\in\tilde{\Theta}(\sigma^{a},\eta)}\dfrac{1}{|\Delta(\sigma^{a})|}\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{|\mathbf{x}|-1}\dfrac{1}{|\Delta((\sigma^{a})^{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})})|}

(51)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mathbf{y}\in\tilde{\Theta}^{W}(\sigma^{a},\eta)}\dfrac{1}{|\Delta(\sigma^{a})\cap W|}\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{|\mathbf{y}|-1}\dfrac{1}{|\Delta((\sigma^{a})^{(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell})})\cap W|}.

For each sequence in
\mathbf{y}\in\tilde{\Theta}^{W}(\sigma^{a},\eta)
, Table
1
gives the probability that this sequence is selected, i.e., the quantity
\dfrac{1}{|\Delta(\sigma^{a}\cap W)|}\prod\limits_{\ell=1}^{|\mathbf{y}|-1}\dfrac{1}{|\Delta((\sigma^{a})^{(y_{1},\ldots,y_{\ell})})\cap W|}
and the state that corresponds to the robust configuration that the sequence leads to. Using Table
1
to evaluate expression (
51
), we obtain expression (
28
).

The remaining transition probabilities can be computed in a similar way. Supporting figures depicting the essential sets and tables listing the relevant sequences of susceptible spins are provided in part A of the Supplementary Material. ∎

5.2.2
Proof of Theorem
3.1

A convenient feature of the auxiliary MDP is the fact that it is not possible for a rectangle of size at least 3x3 to shrink, as by definition of the transition probability kernel, the probability that a state variable that is greater than 3 decreases in the auxiliary MDP is zero. This fact allows us to solve the Bellman equations recursively, starting from state
(N,N)
, which corresponds to the all-plus configuration. Before doing so, we first show the suboptimality of the actions 0 and
\tilde{a}
, such that we can leave these actions out of consideration when solving the Bellman equations.

Lemma 5.7
.

Any stationary deterministic optimal policy
\pi^{*}=(d^{*})^{\infty}
satisfies
d^{*}(i,j)\neq 0
for all
(i,j)\in\hat{S}
,
(i,j)\notin\{(0,0),(N,N)\}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\pi^{*}=(d^{*})^{\infty}
is an optimal policy, with
d^{*}(i,j)=0
for some
(i,j)\in\hat{S}
,
(i,j)\neq(N,N)
. By definition of the transition probabilities and the reward function, this implies
v^{*}(i,j)=0
. Now, let
\pi=d^{\infty}
denote another policy that satisfies
d(i,j)\neq 0
for all
(i,j)\neq(0,0)
. Note that the definition of the transition probabilities imply that for any state
(i,j)\neq(0,0)
, there exists a sequence of states
(s_{1},s_{2},\ldots,s_{k})
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
that satisfies
s_{1}=(i,j)
,
s_{k}=(N,N)
and
\hat{P}(s_{\ell+1}|s_{\ell},d(s_{\ell}))>0)
for all
\ell=1,\ldots,k-1
. From the definition of the reward function, it now follows that
v^{\pi}(i,j)>0
and thus
v^{\pi}(i,j)>v^{*}(i,j)
, which contradicts the optimality of policy
\pi^{*}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 5.8
.

Any stationary deterministic optimal policy
\pi^{*}=(d^{*})^{\infty}
satisfies
d^{*}(i,j)\neq\tilde{a}
for all
(i,j)\in\hat{S}
,
i,j\notin\{0,N\}
.

Proof.

First consider
s=(i,j)\in\hat{S}
, where either
i,j=3,\ldots,N-2
or
(i,j)=(2,2)
. Suppose that
\pi^{*}=(d^{*})^{\infty}
is an optimal policy that satisfies
d^{*}(s)=\tilde{a}
. By expressions (
46
), (
49
) and (
13
), this implies
v^{*}(s)=0
. As shown in the proof of Lemma
5.7
, there exists a policy
\pi=d^{\infty}
that satisfies
v^{\pi}(i,j)>0=v^{*}(i,j)
, which contradicts the optimality of
\pi^{*}
. Hence
d^{*}(s)\neq\tilde{a}
.

We proceed to prove the statement for states
(i,j)\in\hat{S}
, where either
i=2
and
j=3,\ldots,N-2
or
i=3,\ldots,N-2
and
j=2
. Suppose that
\pi^{*}=(d^{*})^{\infty}
is an optimal policy that satisfies
d^{*}(2,j)=\tilde{a}
for some
j=3,\ldots,N-2
. Let
j^{*}
denote the largest such
j
for which this holds. We assume that
3<j^{*}<N-2
. The argument can easily be extended to
j^{*}=3
and
j^{*}=N-2
. Note that rectangles of size
(i,j)
, where
i,j=3,\ldots,N-2,N
or
i=2
,
j=j^{*}+1,\ldots,N-2,N
, cannot shrink under policy
\pi^{*}
. In addition, by the definition of the reward function, the optimality of
\pi^{*}
and the result of Lemma
5.7
, we obtain
v^{*}(i,j)<v^{*}(i+1,j)
and
v^{*}(i,j)<v^{*}(i,j+1)
for
i,j=3,\ldots,N-2,N
or
i=2
,
j=j^{*}+1,\ldots,N-2,N
. We proceed to show that at least one of the following must hold:

1.

d^{*}(2,j^{*}-1)\in\{a_{12}^{\prime},a_{21},a_{21}^{\prime},a_{22},a_{22}^{\prime},a_{0}\}
,

2.

d^{*}(2,j)\in\{a_{21},a_{21}^{\prime},a_{22},a_{22}^{\prime},a_{0}\}
, for some
j=3,\ldots,j^{*}-2
,

3.

d^{*}(2,2)=a_{0}
.

If none of these statements holds, we have
d^{*}(2,j^{*}-1)\in\{a_{11}^{\prime},\tilde{a}\}
,
d^{*}(2,j)\in\{a_{11}^{\prime},a_{12}^{\prime},\tilde{a}\}
and
d^{*}(2,2)\in\{a_{11}^{\prime},a_{12}^{\prime},a_{21}^{\prime},a_{22}^{\prime}\}
. It now follows from the definition of the transition probabilities and from symmetry that the only rectangles that can be reached from state
(2,j^{*})
are those in the set
\{(2,j),(j,2)|j=2,\ldots,j^{*}\}
. By the definition of the reward function, this implies
v^{*}(2,j^{*})=0
. Since there exists a policy
\pi=d^{\infty}
that satisfies
v^{\pi}(s)>0
for all
s\in S\setminus\{(0,0\}
, this contradicts the optimality of
\pi^{*}
. Hence, one of the statements
1-3
must hold. Let
j^{\prime}
denote the largest
j
for which one of these statements is attained. We assume that
j^{\prime}\in\{3,\dots,j^{*}-2\}
and
d^{*}(2,j^{\prime})=a_{21}
. All the remaining scenarios can be handled in a similar way. We construct a policy
\pi=d^{\infty}
, where
d:\hat{S}\rightarrow\hat{A}
is given by

d(i,j)=\begin{cases}a_{21},&\text{if }i=2,\quad j=j^{*},\\
d^{*}(i,j),&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

That is, we replace the action in state
(2,j^{*})
by the action prescribed in state
(2,j^{\prime})
. Now, let
\tau
denote the first hitting time to the state
(2,j^{\prime})
in the process induced by policy
\pi^{*}
, started from state
(2,j^{*})
. Note that
s_{t}\in\{(2,j)|j=j^{\prime}+1,\ldots,j^{*}\}
for all
t<\tau
and that
\tau\geq j^{*}-j^{\prime}
by definition of
\pi^{*}
and the transition probabilities. This implies that
v^{*}(2,j^{*})\leq\lambda^{j^{*}-j^{\prime}}v^{*}(2,j^{\prime})
. Also, by the fact that
d(i,j)=d^{*}(i,j)
for
i,j=3,\ldots,N-2,N
, and for
i=2
,
j=j^{*}+1,\ldots,N-2,N
and the fact that these rectangles cannot shrink, we have
v^{\pi}(i,j)=v^{*}(i,j)
for all
i,j=3,\ldots,N-2,N
and
i=2
,
j=j^{*}+1,\ldots,N-2,N
. We now obtain, using expression (
27
),

v^{*}(2,j^{*})\leq\lambda^{j^{*}-j^{\prime}}v^{*}(2,j^{\prime})=\lambda^{j^{*}-j^{\prime}}\dfrac{2}{3-\lambda}v^{*}(3,j^{\prime}),

and

v^{\pi}(2,j^{*})=\dfrac{2}{3-\lambda}v^{\pi}(3,j^{*})=\dfrac{2}{3-\lambda}v^{*}(3,j^{*}).

Since
v^{*}(3,j^{\prime})<v^{*}(3,j^{*})
, this yields
v^{*}(2,j^{*})<v^{\pi}(2,j^{*})
, contradicting the optimality of
\pi^{*}
. Thus,
d^{*}(i,j)\neq\tilde{a}
for all
(i,j)\in\hat{S}
,
i,j\notin\{0,N\}
.
∎

It follows from Lemmas
5.7
and
5.8
that we can leave actions 0 and
\tilde{a}
out of consideration. The following result implies the suboptimality of actions
a_{11}^{\prime}
,
a_{12}^{\prime}
,
a_{21}^{\prime}
and
a_{22}^{\prime}
. For rectangles with side lengths greater than 2, it shows that these actions are always inferior to their counterparts
a_{11}
,
a_{12}
,
a_{21}
and
a_{22}
respectively. Hence, in finding the optimal policy, when considering a rectangle that has a side of length greater than 2, we leave the corresponding actions in the set
\{a_{11}^{\prime}
,
a_{12}^{\prime}
,
a_{21}^{\prime}
,
a_{22}^{\prime}\}
out of consideration.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
v^{*}:\hat{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
denote the optimal value function of the auxiliary MDP. For any state
s=(x,y)\in\hat{S}
with
3\leq x\leq N-2
and
y\leq N-2
, we have

\hat{r}(s,a_{11}^{\prime})+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{11}^{\prime})v^{*}(s^{\prime})<\hat{r}(s,a_{11})+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{11})v^{*}(s^{\prime}),

(52)

and for any state
s=(x,y)\in\hat{S}
with
3\leq x\leq N-2
and
y\leq N-3
, we have

\hat{r}(s,a_{12}^{\prime})+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{12}^{\prime})v^{*}(s^{\prime})<\hat{r}(s,a_{12})+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{12})v^{*}(s^{\prime}).

(53)

Similarly, for any state
s=(x,y)\in\hat{S}
with
3\leq y\leq N-2
and
x\leq N-2
, we have

\hat{r}(s,a_{21}^{\prime})+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{21}^{\prime})v^{*}(s^{\prime})<\hat{r}(s,a_{21})+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{21})v^{*}(s^{\prime}),

(54)

and for any state
s=(x,y)\in\hat{S}
with
3\leq y\leq N-2
and
x\leq N-3
, we have

\hat{r}(s,a_{22}^{\prime})+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{22}^{\prime})v^{*}(s^{\prime})<\hat{r}(s,a_{22})+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{22})v^{*}(s^{\prime}).

(55)

Proof.

We prove the validity of expressions (
52
) and (
53
). Expressions (
54
) and (
55
) follow immediately by symmetry.
We first consider expression (
52
) for
y=N-2
. The definition of the reward function and expressions (
35
) and (
42
) yield

\hat{r}(s,a_{11}^{\prime})+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{11}^{\prime})v^{*}(s^{\prime})=\dfrac{\lambda}{3}v^{*}(x,N-2)+\dfrac{2\lambda}{3}v^{*}(x,N)

and

\hat{r}(s,a_{11})+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{11})v^{*}(s^{\prime})=\dfrac{\lambda}{4}v^{*}(x,N-2)+\dfrac{3\lambda}{4}v^{*}(x,N).

The facts that
\hat{r}(x,y)=1
if
(x,y)=(N,N)
and
\hat{r}(x,y)=0
otherwise, together with the fact that for each
s\in\hat{S}
,
s\neq(N,N)
, there exists
s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}
,
s^{\prime}\neq s
such that
\hat{P}(s^{\prime}|s,d(s))>0
if
d(s)\neq 0
and the fact that rectangles cannot shrink, imply that
v^{*}(x,y)<v^{*}(x,y+1)
for all
3\leq y\leq N-1
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\dfrac{\lambda}{3}v^{*}(x,N-2)+\dfrac{2\lambda}{3}v^{*}(x,N)

\displaystyle=\left(\dfrac{\lambda}{3}-\dfrac{\lambda}{12}\right)v^{*}(x,N-2)+\dfrac{\lambda}{12}v^{*}(x,N-2)+\dfrac{2\lambda}{3}v^{*}(x,N)

\displaystyle<\dfrac{\lambda}{4}v^{*}(x,N-2)+\dfrac{3\lambda}{4}v^{*}(x,N).

Now suppose that
y<N-2
. Using the definition of the reward function and expressions (
26
) and (
31
), we obtain

\hat{r}(s,a_{11}^{\prime})+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{11}^{\prime})v^{*}(s^{\prime})=\dfrac{\lambda}{2}v^{*}(x,y)+\dfrac{\lambda}{2}v^{*}(x,y+1)

and

\hat{r}(s,a_{11})+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{11})v^{*}(s^{\prime})=\dfrac{\lambda}{3}v^{*}(x,y)+\dfrac{2\lambda}{3}v^{*}(x,y+1).

Again invoking the fact that
v^{*}(x,y)<v^{*}(x,y+1)
for all
3\leq y\leq N-1
, we have

\displaystyle\dfrac{\lambda}{2}v^{*}(x,y)+\dfrac{\lambda}{2}v^{*}(x,y+1)

\displaystyle=\left(\dfrac{\lambda}{2}-\dfrac{\lambda}{6}\right)v^{*}(x,y)+\dfrac{\lambda}{6}v^{*}(x,y)+\dfrac{\lambda}{2}v^{*}(x,y+1)

\displaystyle<\dfrac{\lambda}{3}v^{*}(x,y)+\dfrac{2\lambda}{3}v^{*}(x,y+1),

which proves expression (
52
).

The validity of expression (
53
) can be established in a similar way. We first consider the case
y=N-3
. From the definition of the reward function and expressions (
37
) and (
44
), it follows that

\hat{r}(s,a_{12}^{\prime})+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{12}^{\prime})v^{*}(s^{\prime})=\dfrac{4\lambda}{9}v^{*}(x,N-3)+\dfrac{5\lambda}{27}v^{*}(x,N-2)+\dfrac{10\lambda}{27}v^{*}(x,N)

and

\hat{r}(s,a_{12})+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{12})v^{*}(s^{\prime})=\dfrac{7\lambda}{18}v^{*}(x,N-3)+\dfrac{31\lambda}{144}v^{*}(x,N-2)+\dfrac{19\lambda}{48}v^{*}(x,N).

Again using the fact that
v^{*}(x,y)<v^{*}(x,y+1)
for all
3\leq y\leq N-1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\dfrac{4\lambda}{9}v^{*}(x,N-3)+\dfrac{5\lambda}{27}v^{*}(x,N-2)+\dfrac{10\lambda}{27}v^{*}(x,N)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{4\lambda}{9}v^{*}(x,N-3)+\left(\dfrac{5\lambda}{27}-\dfrac{11\lambda}{432}\right)v^{*}(x,N-2)+\dfrac{11\lambda}{432}v^{*}(x,N-2)+\dfrac{10\lambda}{27}v^{*}(x,N)

\displaystyle<\dfrac{4\lambda}{9}v^{*}(x,N-3)+\dfrac{23\lambda}{144}v^{*}(x,N-2)+\dfrac{19\lambda}{48}v^{*}(x,N)

\displaystyle=\left(\dfrac{4\lambda}{9}-\dfrac{\lambda}{18}\right)v^{*}(x,N-3)+\dfrac{\lambda}{18}v^{*}(x,N-3)+\dfrac{23\lambda}{144}v^{*}(x,N-2)+\dfrac{19\lambda}{48}v^{*}(x,N)

\displaystyle<\dfrac{7\lambda}{18}v^{*}(x,N-3)+\dfrac{31\lambda}{144}v^{*}(x,N-2)+\dfrac{19\lambda}{48}v^{*}(x,N).

Finally, for
y<N-3
, the definition of the reward function and expressions (
28
) and (
33
) yield

\hat{r}(s,a_{12}^{\prime})+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{12}^{\prime})v^{*}(s^{\prime})=\dfrac{5\lambda}{8}v^{*}(x,y)+\dfrac{\lambda}{4}v^{*}(x,y+1)+\dfrac{\lambda}{8}v^{*}(x,y+2)

and

\hat{r}(s,a_{12})+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{12})v^{*}(s^{\prime})=\dfrac{5\lambda}{9}v^{*}(x,y)+\dfrac{7\lambda}{27}v^{*}(x,y+1)+\dfrac{5\lambda}{27}v^{*}(x,y+2).

Invoking once more the fact that
v^{*}(x,y)<v^{*}(x,y+1)
for all
3\leq y\leq N-1
, we conclude

\displaystyle\dfrac{5\lambda}{8}v^{*}(x,y)+\dfrac{\lambda}{4}v^{*}(x,y+1)+\dfrac{\lambda}{8}v^{*}(x,y+2)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{5\lambda}{8}v^{*}(x,y)+\left(\dfrac{\lambda}{4}-\dfrac{13\lambda}{216}\right)v^{*}(x,y+1)+\dfrac{13\lambda}{216}v^{*}(x,y+1)+\dfrac{\lambda}{8}v^{*}(x,y+2)

\displaystyle<\dfrac{5\lambda}{8}v^{*}(x,y)+\dfrac{41\lambda}{216}v^{*}(x,y+1)+\dfrac{5\lambda}{27}v^{*}(x,y+2)

\displaystyle=\left(\dfrac{5\lambda}{8}-\dfrac{5\lambda}{72}\right)v^{*}(x,y)+\dfrac{5\lambda}{72}v^{*}(x,y)+\dfrac{41\lambda}{216}v^{*}(x,y+1)+\dfrac{5\lambda}{27}v^{*}(x,y+2)

\displaystyle<\dfrac{5\lambda}{9}v^{*}(x,y)+\dfrac{7\lambda}{27}v^{*}(x,y+1)+\dfrac{5\lambda}{27}v^{*}(x,y+2).

∎

Lemma
5.9
implies that we can leave actions
a_{11}^{\prime},a_{12}^{\prime},a_{21}^{\prime}
and
a_{22}^{\prime}
out of consideration whenever the corresponding side of the rectangle has length greater than 2. In Lemma
5.10
, we provide a condition under which these actions are suboptimal when the side length equals 2 as well. First, we let
(\hat{S},\hat{A}^{\prime},\hat{P}^{\prime},\hat{r}^{\prime})
denote an MDP that is identical to the auxiliary MDP, apart from the fact that the action spaces of rectangles that have a side of length 2 are extended with the corresponding actions in the set
\{a_{11},a_{12},a_{21}
,
a_{22}\}
. That is,

\hat{A}^{\prime}(i,j)=\begin{cases}\hat{A}(i,j),&\text{if }i,j\neq 2,\\
\hat{A}(i,j)\cup\{a_{11},a_{12}\},&\text{if }i=2,\quad j=3,...,N-3,\\
\hat{A}(i,j)\cup\{a_{11}\},&\text{if }i=2,\quad j=N-2,\\
\hat{A}(i,j)\cup\{a_{21},a_{22}\},&\text{if }i=3,...,N-3,\quad j=2,\\
\hat{A}(i,j)\cup\{a_{21}\},&\text{if }i=N-2,\quad j=2,\\
\hat{A}(i,j)\cup\{a_{11},a_{12},a_{21},a_{22}\},&\text{if }i=j=2.\end{cases}

Let corresponding transition probabilities be defined analogously to expressions (
26
)–(
29
), (
35
)–(
38
), i.e.,

\hat{P}^{\prime}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime})|(2,j),a_{11})=\begin{cases}1/3,&\text{if }i^{\prime}=2,j^{\prime}=j,\\
2/3,&\text{if }i^{\prime}=i,j^{\prime}=j+1,\\
0,&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

for
j=2,\ldots,N-3
in accordance with expression (
26
) and

\hat{P}^{\prime}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime})|(2,N-2),a_{11})=\begin{cases}1/4,&\text{if }i^{\prime}=2,j^{\prime}=N-2,\\
3/4,&\text{if }i^{\prime}=2,j^{\prime}=N,\\
0,&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

in accordance with (
35
). The remaining cases follow from symmetry. Let the reward function be defined analogously to expression (
13
). Lemma
5.10
shows that this artificially extended MDP can provide an easy way to discard actions
a_{11}^{\prime},a_{12}^{\prime},a_{21}^{\prime}
and
a_{22}^{\prime}
for rectangles with side length 2.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
\pi^{*}=(d^{*})^{\infty}
denote an optimal policy in the MDP
(\hat{S},\hat{A},\hat{P},\hat{r})
. Suppose that, for each optimal policy
\hat{\pi}^{*}=(\hat{d}^{*})^{\infty}
in the MDP
(\hat{S},\hat{A}^{\prime},\hat{P}^{\prime},\hat{r}^{\prime})
, all of the following conditions hold:

1.

\hat{d}^{*}(2,j)\notin\{a_{11},a_{12}\}
,
j=3,\ldots,N-3
,

2.

\hat{d}^{*}(2,N-2)\neq a_{11}
,

3.

\hat{d}^{*}(i,2)\notin\{a_{21},a_{22}\}
,
i=3,\ldots,N-3
,

4.

\hat{d}^{*}(N-2,2)\neq a_{21}
,

5.

\hat{d}^{*}(2,2)\notin\{a_{11},a_{12},a_{21},a_{22}\}
.

This implies

1.

d^{*}(2,j)\notin\{a_{11}^{\prime},a_{12}^{\prime}\}
,
j=3,\ldots,N-3
,

2.

d^{*}(2,N-2)\neq a_{11}^{\prime}
,

3.

d^{*}(i,2)\notin\{a_{21}^{\prime},a_{22}^{\prime}\}
,
i=3,\ldots,N-3
,

4.

d^{*}(N-2,2)\neq a_{21}^{\prime}
,

5.

d^{*}(2,2)\notin\{a_{11}^{\prime},a_{12}^{\prime},a_{21}^{\prime},a_{22}^{\prime}\}
.

Proof.

From the fact that none of the actions that were added to
\hat{A}
to obtain the action space
\hat{A}^{\prime}
are optimal in the MDP
(\hat{S},\hat{A}^{\prime},\hat{P}^{\prime},\hat{r})
, it follows that the optimal value function of the latter MDP is equivalent to that of the MDP
(\hat{S},\hat{A},\hat{P},\hat{r})
. We show that this implies
d^{*}(s)\neq a_{11}^{\prime}
for
s=(2,j)
,
j=3,\ldots,N-3
. The remaining cases follow from a similar argument. Since
a_{11}
is not optimal in state
s
in the MDP
(\hat{S},\hat{A}^{\prime},\hat{P}^{\prime},\hat{r})
, there is an action
a^{*}\in\hat{A}^{\prime}(s)\setminus\{a_{11}\}
such that

\hat{r}^{\prime}(s,a_{11})+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}^{\prime}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{11})v^{*}(s^{\prime})<\hat{r}^{\prime}(s,a^{*})+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}^{\prime}(s^{\prime}|s,a^{*})v^{*}(s^{\prime}),

(56)

where
v^{*}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
denotes the optimal value function of both the MDPs. The same arguments that led to Lemma
5.9
can be applied to show that

\hat{r}^{\prime}(s,a_{11}^{\prime})+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}^{\prime}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{11}^{\prime})v^{*}(s^{\prime})<\hat{r}^{\prime}(s,a_{11})+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}^{\prime}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{11})v^{*}(s^{\prime}),

(57)

It now follows from expressions (
56
) and (
57
) that

\hat{r}^{\prime}(s,a_{11}^{\prime})+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}^{\prime}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{11}^{\prime})v^{*}(s^{\prime})<\hat{r}^{\prime}(s,a^{*})+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}^{\prime}(s^{\prime}|s,a^{*})v^{*}(s^{\prime}).

Since this statement does not depend on the presence of action
a_{11}
in the action space
\hat{A}^{\prime}(s)
, we can conclude that for any stationary deterministic optimal policy
\pi^{*}=(d^{*})^{\infty}
in the MDP
(\hat{S},\hat{A},\hat{P},\hat{r})
, we have
d^{*}(2,j)\neq a_{11}^{\prime}
,
j=3,\ldots,N-3
.
∎

Now that actions
0
,
\tilde{a}
,
a_{11}^{\prime}
,
a_{21}^{\prime}
,
a_{12}^{\prime}
and
a_{22}^{\prime}
are dealt with, we unravel the structure of the optimal policy in the auxiliary MDP, which is specified in Theorem
3.1
. Figure
2
provides a visualization of this result.

Proof of Theorem
3.1

We show that the value function
v^{\pi^{*}}_{\lambda}:\hat{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
of a stationary, deterministic policy
\pi^{*}=(d^{*})^{\infty}
satisfies the Bellman optimality equations (
2
) if and only if it has the specified form. The statement then follows immediately from Theorem
2.1
, since the state and action spaces are both finite. Note that it suffices to consider states of the form
(i,j)
,
i\geq j
. The analogous results for states of the form
(i,j)
,
i<j
follow directly from symmetry. To obtain the desired result for states
(i,2)
,
i=2,\ldots,N-2
(resp.
(2,j)
,
j=2,\ldots,N-2
), we prove the statement for the MDP
(\hat{S},\hat{A}^{\prime},\hat{P}^{\prime},\hat{r}^{\prime})
. The result for the original auxiliary MDP
(\hat{S},\hat{A},\hat{P},\hat{r})
then follows immediately from Lemma
5.10
.

Let
\Pi_{1}
denote the space of stationary, deterministic policies
\pi_{1}=d_{1}^{\infty}
that satisfy
d_{1}(i,j)\in A^{*}_{1}(i,j)
for all
(i,j)\in\hat{S}
. Similarly, let
\Pi_{2}
denote the space of stationary, deterministic policies
\pi_{2}=d_{2}^{\infty}
that satisfy
d_{2}(i,j)\in A^{*}_{2}(i,j)
for all
(i,j)\in\hat{S}
. We proceed to show that
v^{\pi^{*}}_{\lambda}:\hat{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
for
\lambda\in(\lambda_{c},1)
satisfies the Bellman optimality equations if and only if
\pi^{*}\in\Pi_{1}
, that
v^{\pi^{*}}_{\lambda}:\hat{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
for
\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{c})
satisfies the Bellman optimality equations if and only if
\pi^{*}\in\Pi_{2}
and that
v^{\pi^{*}}_{\lambda_{c}}:\hat{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfies the Bellman optimality equations if and only if
\pi^{*}\in\Pi_{1}\cup\Pi_{2}
.

To this end, let
\pi_{1}=d_{1}^{\infty}
and
\pi_{2}=d_{2}^{\infty}
be two policies in
\Pi_{1}
and
\Pi_{2}
respectively. Note that
A^{*}_{k}(i,j)
,
k=1,2
is a singleton for all
(i,j)\in\hat{S}
,
(i,j)\neq(N-3,N-3)
. Since actions
a_{12}
and
a_{22}
have the same effect in state
(N-3,N-3)
, we let
d_{1}(N-3,N-3)=d_{2}(N-3,N-3)=a_{22}
without loss of generality. Using the transition probabilities (
26
–
45
), we obtain the following recursive expressions for
v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{1}}
and
v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{2}}
:

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N,N)=\dfrac{1}{1-\lambda},\quad k=1,2,

(58)

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N,N-2)=\dfrac{3\lambda}{4-\lambda}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N,N),\quad k=1,2,

(59)

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N,N-3)=\dfrac{31\lambda}{8(18-7\lambda)}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N,N-2)+\dfrac{57\lambda}{8(18-7\lambda)}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N,N),\quad k=1,2,

(60)

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{1}}(N,j)=\dfrac{2\lambda}{3-\lambda}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{1}}(N,j+1),\quad j=2,\ldots,N-4,

(61)

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{2}}(N,j)=\begin{cases}\dfrac{2\lambda}{3-\lambda}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{2}}(N,j+1),&\text{if }j=N-4,\\
\dfrac{7\lambda}{3(9-5\lambda)}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{2}}(N,j+1)+\dfrac{5\lambda}{3(9-5\lambda)}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{2}}(N,j+2),&\text{if }j=2,\ldots,N-5,\end{cases}

(62)

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N-2,N-2)=\dfrac{9\lambda}{2(18-7\lambda)}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N,N-2)+\dfrac{13\lambda}{2(18-7\lambda)}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N,N),\quad k=1,2,

(63)

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N-2,N-3)=\dfrac{31\lambda}{8(18-7\lambda)}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N-2,N-2)+\dfrac{57\lambda}{8(18-7\lambda)}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N-2,N),\quad k=1,2,

(64)

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N-2,j)=\dfrac{12}{12-5\lambda}\left(\dfrac{\lambda}{8}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N,j)+\dfrac{25\lambda}{72}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N,j+1)+\dfrac{\lambda}{9}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N-2,j+1)\right),

(65)

\displaystyle\quad j=2,\ldots,N-4,\quad k=1,2,

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N-3,N-3)=\dfrac{31\lambda}{8(18-7\lambda)}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N-2,N-3)+\dfrac{57\lambda}{8(18-7\lambda)}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N,N-3),\quad k=1,2,

(66)

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(i,j)=\dfrac{\lambda}{9-4\lambda}\Big(v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(i,j+1)+v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(i+1,j)+3v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(i+1,j+1)\Big),

(67)

\displaystyle\quad i=2,\ldots,N-3,\quad j=2,\ldots,N-4,\quad k=1,2.

Analogous expressions for states of the form
(i,j)
,

follow directly from symmetry. Note that expressions (

–

) together with Corollary

allow us to compute the quantity
\mathbb{E}[\lambda^{\tau^{(i,j),\pi_{k}}_{(N,N)}}]
for

,
(i,j)\in\hat{S}
.
It suffices to show that for any state
s=(i,j)\in\hat{S}
, we have

\hat{r}(s,a)+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a)v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{1}}(s^{\prime})<\hat{r}(s,d_{1}(s))+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}(s^{\prime}|s,d_{1}(s))v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{1}}(s^{\prime}),

(68)

for all

,

\hat{r}(s,a)+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a)v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{2}}(s^{\prime})<\hat{r}(s,d_{2}(s))+\lambda\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}(s^{\prime}|s,d_{2}(s))v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{2}}(s^{\prime}),

(69)

for all

and

\displaystyle v_{\lambda_{c}}^{\pi_{1}}(s)

\displaystyle=v_{\lambda_{c}}^{\pi_{2}}(s):=v_{\lambda_{c}}(s),

(70)

\displaystyle\hat{r}(s,d_{1}(s))+\lambda_{c}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}(s^{\prime}|s,d_{1}(s))v_{\lambda_{c}}(s^{\prime})

\displaystyle>\hat{r}(s,a)+\lambda_{c}\sum\limits_{s^{\prime}\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}(s^{\prime}|s,a)v_{\lambda_{c}}(s^{\prime}),

for all

Assuming that

,

\displaystyle\lambda\sum\limits_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime})|(N,N-3),a_{12})v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})>\lambda\sum\limits_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime})|(N,N-3),a_{11})v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\quad\text{for }k=1,\lambda\in[\lambda_{c},1)\text{ and }k=2,\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{c}],

\displaystyle\lambda\sum\limits_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime})|(N,N-4),a_{11})v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})>\lambda\sum\limits_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime})|(N,N-4),a_{12})v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\quad\text{for }k=1,\lambda\in[\lambda_{c},1)\text{ and }k=2,\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{c}],

\displaystyle\lambda\sum\limits_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime})|(N,j),a_{11})v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{1}}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})>\lambda\sum\limits_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime})|(N,j),a_{12})v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{1}}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\quad\text{for }\lambda\in(\lambda^{c},1),\quad j=2,\ldots,N-5,

\displaystyle\lambda\sum\limits_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime})|(i,j),a_{12})v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{2}}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})>\lambda\sum\limits_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime})|(i,j),a_{11})v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{2}}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\quad\text{for }\lambda\in(0,\lambda^{c})\text{ and }j=2,\ldots,N-5,\text{ and }

\displaystyle\lambda_{c}\sum\limits_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime})|(N,j),a_{11})v_{\lambda_{c}}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})=\lambda_{c}\sum\limits_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in\hat{S}}\hat{P}((i^{\prime},j^{\prime})|(N,j),a_{12})v_{\lambda_{c}}(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}),

(71)

\displaystyle-6v^{\pi_{k}}_{\lambda}(N,N-4)+11v^{\pi_{k}}_{\lambda}(N,N-3)-5v^{\pi_{k}}_{\lambda}(N,N-2)>0,\quad k=1,\lambda\in[\lambda_{c},1)\text{ and }k=2,\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{c}],

(73)

\displaystyle 6v^{\pi_{2}}_{\lambda}(N,j)-11v^{\pi_{2}}_{\lambda}(N,j+1)+5v^{\pi_{2}}_{\lambda}(N,j+2)>0,\quad\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{c}),\quad j=2,\ldots,N-5,\text{ and }

\displaystyle-6v_{\lambda_{c}}(N,j)+11v_{\lambda_{c}}(N,j+1)-5v_{\lambda_{c}}(N,j+2)=0,\quad j=2,\ldots,N-5.

for

Thus, in order to obtain the desired result, it suffices to prove that the value functions of policies
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
satisfy expressions (
71
–
92
), in addition to the equations
v^{\pi_{1}}_{\lambda^{c}}(N,j)=v^{\pi_{2}}_{\lambda^{c}}(N,j)
for all
j=2,\ldots,N-2,N
. To prove the set of (in)equalities, we make use of the recursive expressions (
58
–
67
) for
v^{\pi_{1}}_{\lambda}
and
v^{\pi_{2}}_{\lambda}
. The proof crucially relies on the fact that rectangles of size at least
3\times 3
cannot shrink under the dynamics of the auxiliary MDP, which allows us to show the validity of the expressions by means of backward induction over the size of the rectangle. For states of the form
(N,j)
,
(N-2,j)
and
(N-3,j)
,
j=2,\ldots,N
, we use backward induction over the length of the vertical side of the rectangle. For states of the form
(i,j)
,
i,j=2,\ldots,N-4
, i.e., expressions (
89
–
92
), we invoke a more involved induction argument, which can be outlined as follows:

•

Induction base:
First, we show that the expressions are satisfied for states of the form
(i,N-4)
and
(N-4,j)
,
i,j=2,\ldots,N-4
, using an embedded induction argument over the length of the shortest side of the rectangle.

•

Induction hypothesis
: We assume that the expressions are valid for states of the form
(i,n+1)
and
(n+1,j)
,
i,j=2,\ldots,n+1
for some
n<N-4
.

•

Induction step:
First, we show that the induction hypothesis implies the correctness of the expressions for state
(n,n)
. Using this result, together with the induction hypothesis, we invoke another embedded induction argument over the length of the shortest side of the rectangle to show that the expressions hold for states of the form
(i,n)
and
(n,j)
,
i,j=2,\ldots,n-1
.

The details of the induction proofs are provided in part B of the Supplementary Material. ∎

6
Discussion

In this paper, we have introduced the spatiotemporal Markov decision process (STMDP), a new framework for sequential decision making problems that exhibit not only temporal, but also spatial interaction structures. Unlike the related FMDP and GMDP frameworks, the class of STMDPs includes processes that do not permit a factorisation of the state space. We have formulated and analyzed an STMDP inspired by the low-temperature Ising model on a finite, two-dimensional, square lattice, evolving according to the asynchronous Metropolis dynamics. Our analysis heavily relied on a reduction of the state space to local minima of the Hamiltonian, resulting in an auxiliary MDP. We argued that these local minima indeed make the largest contribution to the Bellman optimality equations if the adjustment time is sufficiently long. For the auxiliary MDP, we uncovered the structure of the exact optimal policy by solving the Bellman optimality equations in a recursive manner. Finally, we conducted numerical experiments on the performance of the analogue of this optimal policy in the original STMDP and compared it to the performance of alternative policies. The results of these experiments suggest that this policy obtained from the auxiliary MDP achieves the best performance over a range of different adjustment times.

This work opens several interesting avenues for future research. First of all, a more rigorous analysis of the Ising STMDP for small values of the adjustment time could be performed, where the policy obtained from the auxiliary MDP may no longer be optimal. In addition, the Ising STMDP for higher temperatures would be a worthwile object of study, although much more difficult to handle. Furthermore, a potential direction would be to analyze the Ising STMDP for different starting and/or target configurations, for example consisting of multiple clusters of different shapes. Additionally, it would be of interest to study the Ising STMDP on the infinite lattice, in different dimensions or on different types of graphs. Finally, the idea of reducing the state space to local minima of the Hamiltonian may yield insights for a more general class of STMDPs, based on dynamics that are reversible with respect to a Gibbs measure.

Supplementary Material B: Details of the proof of Theorem 3.1

Let policies
\pi_{1}=d_{1}^{\infty}
and
\pi_{2}=d_{2}^{\infty}
denote two arbitrary policies in
\Pi_{1}
and
\Pi_{2}
respectively. Recall that the value functions
v^{\pi_{1}}_{\lambda}:\hat{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
v^{\pi_{2}}_{\lambda}:\hat{S}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfy the following recursive expressions:

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N,N)=\dfrac{1}{1-\lambda},\quad k=1,2,

(93)

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N,N-2)=\dfrac{3\lambda}{4-\lambda}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N,N),\quad k=1,2,

(94)

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N,N-3)=\dfrac{31\lambda}{8(18-7\lambda)}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N,N-2)+\dfrac{57\lambda}{8(18-7\lambda)}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N,N),\quad k=1,2,

(95)

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{1}}(N,j)=\dfrac{2\lambda}{3-\lambda}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{1}}(N,j+1),\quad j=2,\ldots,N-4,

(96)

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{2}}(N,j)=\begin{cases}\dfrac{2\lambda}{3-\lambda}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{2}}(N,j+1),&\text{if }j=N-4,\\
\\
\dfrac{7\lambda}{3(9-5\lambda)}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{2}}(N,j+1)+\dfrac{5\lambda}{3(9-5\lambda)}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{2}}(N,j+2),&\text{if }j=2,\ldots,N-5,\end{cases}

(97)

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N-2,N-2)=\dfrac{9\lambda}{2(18-7\lambda)}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N,N-2)+\dfrac{13\lambda}{2(18-7\lambda)}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N,N),\quad k=1,2,

(98)

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N-2,N-3)=\dfrac{31\lambda}{8(18-7\lambda)}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N-2,N-2)+\dfrac{57\lambda}{8(18-7\lambda)}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N-2,N),\quad k=1,2,

(99)

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N-2,j)=\dfrac{12}{12-5\lambda}\Big(\dfrac{\lambda}{8}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N,j)+\dfrac{25\lambda}{72}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N,j+1)+\dfrac{\lambda}{9}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N-2,j+1)\Big),

(100)

\displaystyle\quad j=2,\ldots,N-4,\quad k=1,2,

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N-3,N-3)=\dfrac{31\lambda}{8(18-7\lambda)}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N-2,N-3)+\dfrac{57\lambda}{8(18-7\lambda)}v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(N,N-3),\quad k=1,2,

(101)

\displaystyle v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(i,j)=\dfrac{\lambda}{9-4\lambda}\Big(v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(i,j+1)+v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(i+1,j)+3v_{\lambda}^{\pi_{k}}(i+1,j+1)\Big),

(102)

\displaystyle\quad i=2,\ldots,N-3,\quad j=2,\ldots,N-4,\quad k=1,2.

In Table
14
, explicit expressions for these value functions are recorded for several states, which will be required at a later point in the proof. Using expressions (

-

), we show that
v^{\pi_{1}}_{\lambda}
and
v^{\pi_{2}}_{\lambda}
satisfy
v^{\pi_{1}}_{\lambda^{c}}(N,j)=v^{\pi_{2}}_{\lambda^{c}}(N,j):=v_{\lambda^{c}}(N,j)
for all

and expressions (

,

,

-

,

), which are recollected below:

\displaystyle 8v^{\pi_{k}}_{\lambda}(N,N-3)-65v^{\pi_{k}}_{\lambda}(N,N-2)+57v^{\pi_{k}}_{\lambda}(N,N)>0,\quad\text{for }k=1,\lambda\in[\lambda_{c},1)\text{ and }k=2,\lambda\in(0,\lambda_{c}],

(103)
